CODE SMELL DETAILS
src\main\java\org\json\CDL.java:26
  Code smell: UseUtilityClass
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: All methods are static. Consider adding a private no-args constructor to prevent instantiation.
  Rule explanation: For classes that only have static methods, consider making them utility classes. Note that this doesn't apply to abstract classes, since their subclasses may well include non-static methods. Also, if you want this class to be a utility class, remember to add a private constructor to prevent instantiation. (Note, that this use was known before PMD 5.1.0 as UseSingleton).
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#useutilityclass

src\main\java\org\json\CDL.java:26
  Code smell: TooManyMethods
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: This class has too many methods, consider refactoring it.
  Rule explanation: A class with too many methods is probably a good suspect for refactoring, in order to reduce its complexity and find a way to have more fine grained objects.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#toomanymethods

src\main\java\org\json\CDL.java:44
  Code smell: CognitiveComplexity
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: The method 'getValue(JSONTokener, char)' has a cognitive complexity of 24, current threshold is 15
  Rule explanation: Methods that are highly complex are difficult to read and more costly to maintain. If you include too much decisional logic within a single method, you make its behavior hard to understand and more difficult to modify. Cognitive complexity is a measure of how difficult it is for humans to read and understand a method. Code that contains a break in the control flow is more complex, whereas the use of language shorthands doesn't increase the level of complexity. Nested control flows can make a method more difficult to understand, with each additional nesting of the control flow leading to an increase in cognitive complexity. Information about Cognitive complexity can be found in the original paper here: <https://www.sonarsource.com/docs/CognitiveComplexity.pdf> By default, this rule reports methods with a complexity of 15 or more. Reported methods should be broken down into less complex components.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#cognitivecomplexity

src\main\java\org\json\CDL.java:44
  Code smell: CyclomaticComplexity
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: The method 'getValue(JSONTokener, char)' has a cyclomatic complexity of 15.
  Rule explanation: The complexity of methods directly affects maintenance costs and readability. Concentrating too much decisional logic in a single method makes its behaviour hard to read and change. Cyclomatic complexity assesses the complexity of a method by counting the number of decision points in a method, plus one for the method entry. Decision points are places where the control flow jumps to another place in the program. As such, they include all control flow statements, such as `if`, `while`, `for`, and `case`. For more details on the calculation, see the documentation {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#CYCLO %}. Generally, numbers ranging from 1-4 denote low complexity, 5-7 denote moderate complexity, 8-10 denote high complexity, and 11+ is very high complexity. By default, this rule reports methods with a complexity >= 10. Additionally, classes with many methods of moderate complexity get reported as well once the total of their methods' complexities reaches 80, even if none of the methods was directly reported. Reported methods should be broken down into several smaller methods. Reported classes should probably be broken down into subcomponents.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#cyclomaticcomplexity

src\main\java\org\json\CDL.java:44
  Code smell: AvoidUncheckedExceptionsInSignatures
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: A method or constructor should not explicitly declare unchecked exception JSONException in its 'throws' clause
  Rule explanation: Reports unchecked exceptions in the `throws` clause of a method or constructor. Java doesn't force the caller to handle an unchecked exception, so it's unnecessary except for documentation. A better practice is to document the exceptional cases with a `@throws` Javadoc tag, which allows being more descriptive.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#avoiduncheckedexceptionsinsignatures

  Code smell: AvoidDeeplyNestedIfStmts
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: Deeply nested if..then statements are hard to read
  Rule explanation: Avoid creating deeply nested if-then statements since they are harder to read and error-prone to maintain.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#avoiddeeplynestedifstmts

  Why: All methods are static. Consider adding a private no-args constructor to prevent instantiation.
  Rule explanation: For classes that only have static methods, consider making them utility classes. Note that this doesn't apply to abstract classes, since their subclasses may well include non-static methods. Also, if you want this class to be a utility class, remember to add a private constructor to prevent instantiation. (Note, that this use was known before PMD 5.1.0 as UseSingleton).
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#useutilityclass

src\main\java\org\json\CookieList.java:35
  Code smell: AvoidUncheckedExceptionsInSignatures
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: A method or constructor should not explicitly declare unchecked exception JSONException in its 'throws' clause
  Rule explanation: Reports unchecked exceptions in the `throws` clause of a method or constructor. Java doesn't force the caller to handle an unchecked exception, so it's unnecessary except for documentation. A better practice is to document the exceptional cases with a `@throws` Javadoc tag, which allows being more descriptive.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#avoiduncheckedexceptionsinsignatures

src\main\java\org\json\HTTP.java:113
  Code smell: CyclomaticComplexity
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: The method 'toString(JSONObject)' has a cyclomatic complexity of 13.
  Rule explanation: The complexity of methods directly affects maintenance costs and readability. Concentrating too much decisional logic in a single method makes its behaviour hard to read and change. Cyclomatic complexity assesses the complexity of a method by counting the number of decision points in a method, plus one for the method entry. Decision points are places where the control flow jumps to another place in the program. As such, they include all control flow statements, such as `if`, `while`, `for`, and `case`. For more details on the calculation, see the documentation {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#CYCLO %}. Generally, numbers ranging from 1-4 denote low complexity, 5-7 denote moderate complexity, 8-10 denote high complexity, and 11+ is very high complexity. By default, this rule reports methods with a complexity >= 10. Additionally, classes with many methods of moderate complexity get reported as well once the total of their methods' complexities reaches 80, even if none of the methods was directly reported. Reported methods should be broken down into several smaller methods. Reported classes should probably be broken down into subcomponents.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#cyclomaticcomplexity

src\main\java\org\json\HTTPTokener.java:29
  Code smell: CognitiveComplexity
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: The method 'nextToken()' has a cognitive complexity of 15, current threshold is 15
  Rule explanation: Methods that are highly complex are difficult to read and more costly to maintain. If you include too much decisional logic within a single method, you make its behavior hard to understand and more difficult to modify. Cognitive complexity is a measure of how difficult it is for humans to read and understand a method. Code that contains a break in the control flow is more complex, whereas the use of language shorthands doesn't increase the level of complexity. Nested control flows can make a method more difficult to understand, with each additional nesting of the control flow leading to an increase in cognitive complexity. Information about Cognitive complexity can be found in the original paper here: <https://www.sonarsource.com/docs/CognitiveComplexity.pdf> By default, this rule reports methods with a complexity of 15 or more. Reported methods should be broken down into less complex components.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#cognitivecomplexity

src\main\java\org\json\JSONArray.java:63
  Code smell: GodClass
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: Possible God Class (WMC=273, ATFD=19, TCC=2.745%)
  Rule explanation: The God Class rule detects the God Class design flaw using metrics. God classes do too many things, are very big and overly complex. They should be split apart to be more object-oriented. The rule uses the detection strategy described in "Object-Oriented Metrics in Practice". The violations are reported against the entire class. The rule uses metrics to implement its detection strategy. The violation message gives information about the values of these metrics: * WMC: a class complexity measure, see {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#WEIGHED_METHOD_COUNT %} * ATFD: a measure of how much data external data the class uses, see {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#ACCESS_TO_FOREIGN_DATA %} * TCC: a measure of how tightly related the methods are, see {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#TIGHT_CLASS_COHESION %} The rule identifies a god class by looking for classes which have all of the following properties: * High WMC * High ATFD * Low TCC See also the reference: Michele Lanza and Radu Marinescu. *Object-Oriented Metrics in Practice: Using Software Metrics to Characterize, Evaluate, and Improve the Design of Object-Oriented Systems.* Springer, Berlin, 1 edition, October 2006. Page 80.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#godclass

src\main\java\org\json\JSONArray.java:378
  Code smell: AvoidCatchingGenericException
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: Avoid catching Exception in try-catch block
  Rule explanation: Avoid catching generic exceptions in try-catch blocks. Catching overly broad exception types makes it difficult to understand what can actually go wrong in your code and can hide real problems. **Why these exceptions should not be caught:** * **Exception**: This is the base class for all checked exceptions. Catching it means you're handling all possible checked exceptions the same way, which is rarely appropriate and makes error handling less precise. * **RuntimeException**: These represent programming errors (like logic bugs) that should typically be fixed in code rather than caught and handled. Catching them can hide bugs that should be addressed during development. * **NullPointerException**: This usually indicates a programming error (accessing null references). Rather than catching it, code should be written to avoid null pointer dereferences through null checks or defensive programming. * **Throwable**: This is the superclass of all errors and exceptions. Catching it means you're trying to handle both recoverable exceptions and serious errors (like OutOfMemoryError) the same way, which is dangerous. * **Error**: These represent serious problems that applications should not try to handle (like OutOfMemoryError, StackOverflowError). Catching Error can prevent the JVM from properly terminating when it encounters unrecoverable situations. **Better approaches:** - Catch specific exception types that you can meaningfully handle - Use multiple catch blocks for different exception types that require different handling - Consider using defensive programming techniques to prevent exceptions rather than catching them
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_errorprone.html#avoidcatchinggenericexception

  Why: A method or constructor should not explicitly declare unchecked exception JSONException in its 'throws' clause
  Rule explanation: Reports unchecked exceptions in the `throws` clause of a method or constructor. Java doesn't force the caller to handle an unchecked exception, so it's unnecessary except for documentation. A better practice is to document the exceptional cases with a `@throws` Javadoc tag, which allows being more descriptive.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#avoiduncheckedexceptionsinsignatures

src\main\java\org\json\JSONArray.java:510
  Code smell: AvoidCatchingGenericException
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: Avoid catching Exception in try-catch block
  Rule explanation: Avoid catching generic exceptions in try-catch blocks. Catching overly broad exception types makes it difficult to understand what can actually go wrong in your code and can hide real problems. **Why these exceptions should not be caught:** * **Exception**: This is the base class for all checked exceptions. Catching it means you're handling all possible checked exceptions the same way, which is rarely appropriate and makes error handling less precise. * **RuntimeException**: These represent programming errors (like logic bugs) that should typically be fixed in code rather than caught and handled. Catching them can hide bugs that should be addressed during development. * **NullPointerException**: This usually indicates a programming error (accessing null references). Rather than catching it, code should be written to avoid null pointer dereferences through null checks or defensive programming. * **Throwable**: This is the superclass of all errors and exceptions. Catching it means you're trying to handle both recoverable exceptions and serious errors (like OutOfMemoryError) the same way, which is dangerous. * **Error**: These represent serious problems that applications should not try to handle (like OutOfMemoryError, StackOverflowError). Catching Error can prevent the JVM from properly terminating when it encounters unrecoverable situations. **Better approaches:** - Catch specific exception types that you can meaningfully handle - Use multiple catch blocks for different exception types that require different handling - Consider using defensive programming techniques to prevent exceptions rather than catching them
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_errorprone.html#avoidcatchinggenericexception

src\main\java\org\json\JSONArray.java:568
  Code smell: AvoidCatchingGenericException
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: Avoid catching Exception in try-catch block
  Rule explanation: Avoid catching generic exceptions in try-catch blocks. Catching overly broad exception types makes it difficult to understand what can actually go wrong in your code and can hide real problems. **Why these exceptions should not be caught:** * **Exception**: This is the base class for all checked exceptions. Catching it means you're handling all possible checked exceptions the same way, which is rarely appropriate and makes error handling less precise. * **RuntimeException**: These represent programming errors (like logic bugs) that should typically be fixed in code rather than caught and handled. Catching them can hide bugs that should be addressed during development. * **NullPointerException**: This usually indicates a programming error (accessing null references). Rather than catching it, code should be written to avoid null pointer dereferences through null checks or defensive programming. * **Throwable**: This is the superclass of all errors and exceptions. Catching it means you're trying to handle both recoverable exceptions and serious errors (like OutOfMemoryError) the same way, which is dangerous. * **Error**: These represent serious problems that applications should not try to handle (like OutOfMemoryError, StackOverflowError). Catching Error can prevent the JVM from properly terminating when it encounters unrecoverable situations. **Better approaches:** - Catch specific exception types that you can meaningfully handle - Use multiple catch blocks for different exception types that require different handling - Consider using defensive programming techniques to prevent exceptions rather than catching them
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_errorprone.html#avoidcatchinggenericexception

src\main\java\org\json\JSONArray.java:1659
  Code smell: CognitiveComplexity
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: The method 'isSimilar(Object, Object)' has a cognitive complexity of 18, current threshold is 15
  Rule explanation: Methods that are highly complex are difficult to read and more costly to maintain. If you include too much decisional logic within a single method, you make its behavior hard to understand and more difficult to modify. Cognitive complexity is a measure of how difficult it is for humans to read and understand a method. Code that contains a break in the control flow is more complex, whereas the use of language shorthands doesn't increase the level of complexity. Nested control flows can make a method more difficult to understand, with each additional nesting of the control flow leading to an increase in cognitive complexity. Information about Cognitive complexity can be found in the original paper here: <https://www.sonarsource.com/docs/CognitiveComplexity.pdf> By default, this rule reports methods with a complexity of 15 or more. Reported methods should be broken down into less complex components.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#cognitivecomplexity

src\main\java\org\json\JSONArray.java:1659
  Code smell: CyclomaticComplexity
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: The method 'isSimilar(Object, Object)' has a cyclomatic complexity of 12.
  Rule explanation: The complexity of methods directly affects maintenance costs and readability. Concentrating too much decisional logic in a single method makes its behaviour hard to read and change. Cyclomatic complexity assesses the complexity of a method by counting the number of decision points in a method, plus one for the method entry. Decision points are places where the control flow jumps to another place in the program. As such, they include all control flow statements, such as `if`, `while`, `for`, and `case`. For more details on the calculation, see the documentation {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#CYCLO %}. Generally, numbers ranging from 1-4 denote low complexity, 5-7 denote moderate complexity, 8-10 denote high complexity, and 11+ is very high complexity. By default, this rule reports methods with a complexity >= 10. Additionally, classes with many methods of moderate complexity get reported as well once the total of their methods' complexities reaches 80, even if none of the methods was directly reported. Reported methods should be broken down into several smaller methods. Reported classes should probably be broken down into subcomponents.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#cyclomaticcomplexity

src\main\java\org\json\JSONArray.java:1806
  Code smell: AvoidUncheckedExceptionsInSignatures
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: A method or constructor should not explicitly declare unchecked exception JSONException in its 'throws' clause
  Rule explanation: Reports unchecked exceptions in the `throws` clause of a method or constructor. Java doesn't force the caller to handle an unchecked exception, so it's unnecessary except for documentation. A better practice is to document the exceptional cases with a `@throws` Javadoc tag, which allows being more descriptive.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#avoiduncheckedexceptionsinsignatures

src\main\java\org\json\JSONArray.java:1855
  Code smell: AvoidCatchingGenericException
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: Avoid catching Exception in try-catch block
  Rule explanation: Avoid catching generic exceptions in try-catch blocks. Catching overly broad exception types makes it difficult to understand what can actually go wrong in your code and can hide real problems. **Why these exceptions should not be caught:** * **Exception**: This is the base class for all checked exceptions. Catching it means you're handling all possible checked exceptions the same way, which is rarely appropriate and makes error handling less precise. * **RuntimeException**: These represent programming errors (like logic bugs) that should typically be fixed in code rather than caught and handled. Catching them can hide bugs that should be addressed during development. * **NullPointerException**: This usually indicates a programming error (accessing null references). Rather than catching it, code should be written to avoid null pointer dereferences through null checks or defensive programming. * **Throwable**: This is the superclass of all errors and exceptions. Catching it means you're trying to handle both recoverable exceptions and serious errors (like OutOfMemoryError) the same way, which is dangerous. * **Error**: These represent serious problems that applications should not try to handle (like OutOfMemoryError, StackOverflowError). Catching Error can prevent the JVM from properly terminating when it encounters unrecoverable situations. **Better approaches:** - Catch specific exception types that you can meaningfully handle - Use multiple catch blocks for different exception types that require different handling - Consider using defensive programming techniques to prevent exceptions rather than catching them
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_errorprone.html#avoidcatchinggenericexception

  Code smell: UseUtilityClass
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: All methods are static. Consider adding a private no-args constructor to prevent instantiation.
  Rule explanation: For classes that only have static methods, consider making them utility classes. Note that this doesn't apply to abstract classes, since their subclasses may well include non-static methods. Also, if you want this class to be a utility class, remember to add a private constructor to prevent instantiation. (Note, that this use was known before PMD 5.1.0 as UseSingleton).
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#useutilityclass

src\main\java\org\json\JSONML.java:15
  Code smell: GodClass
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: Possible God Class (WMC=110, ATFD=8, TCC=0.000%)
  Rule explanation: The God Class rule detects the God Class design flaw using metrics. God classes do too many things, are very big and overly complex. They should be split apart to be more object-oriented. The rule uses the detection strategy described in "Object-Oriented Metrics in Practice". The violations are reported against the entire class. The rule uses metrics to implement its detection strategy. The violation message gives information about the values of these metrics: * WMC: a class complexity measure, see {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#WEIGHED_METHOD_COUNT %} * ATFD: a measure of how much data external data the class uses, see {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#ACCESS_TO_FOREIGN_DATA %} * TCC: a measure of how tightly related the methods are, see {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#TIGHT_CLASS_COHESION %} The rule identifies a god class by looking for classes which have all of the following properties: * High WMC * High ATFD * Low TCC See also the reference: Michele Lanza and Radu Marinescu. *Object-Oriented Metrics in Practice: Using Software Metrics to Characterize, Evaluate, and Improve the Design of Object-Oriented Systems.* Springer, Berlin, 1 edition, October 2006. Page 80.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#godclass

src\main\java\org\json\JSONML.java:15
  Code smell: CyclomaticComplexity
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: The class 'JSONML' has a total cyclomatic complexity of 110 (highest 65).
  Rule explanation: The complexity of methods directly affects maintenance costs and readability. Concentrating too much decisional logic in a single method makes its behaviour hard to read and change. Cyclomatic complexity assesses the complexity of a method by counting the number of decision points in a method, plus one for the method entry. Decision points are places where the control flow jumps to another place in the program. As such, they include all control flow statements, such as `if`, `while`, `for`, and `case`. For more details on the calculation, see the documentation {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#CYCLO %}. Generally, numbers ranging from 1-4 denote low complexity, 5-7 denote moderate complexity, 8-10 denote high complexity, and 11+ is very high complexity. By default, this rule reports methods with a complexity >= 10. Additionally, classes with many methods of moderate complexity get reported as well once the total of their methods' complexities reaches 80, even if none of the methods was directly reported. Reported methods should be broken down into several smaller methods. Reported classes should probably be broken down into subcomponents.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#cyclomaticcomplexity

  Why: This class has too many methods, consider refactoring it.
  Rule explanation: A class with too many methods is probably a good suspect for refactoring, in order to reduce its complexity and find a way to have more fine grained objects.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#toomanymethods

  Rule explanation: This rule uses the NCSS (Non-Commenting Source Statements) metric to determine the number of lines of code in a class, method or constructor. NCSS ignores comments, blank lines, and only counts actual statements. For more details on the calculation, see the documentation {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#NCSS %}.

src\main\java\org\json\JSONML.java:87
  Code smell: CognitiveComplexity
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: The method 'parse(XMLTokener, boolean, JSONArray, JSONMLParserConfiguration, int)' has a cognitive complexity of 218, current threshold is 15
  Rule explanation: Methods that are highly complex are difficult to read and more costly to maintain. If you include too much decisional logic within a single method, you make its behavior hard to understand and more difficult to modify. Cognitive complexity is a measure of how difficult it is for humans to read and understand a method. Code that contains a break in the control flow is more complex, whereas the use of language shorthands doesn't increase the level of complexity. Nested control flows can make a method more difficult to understand, with each additional nesting of the control flow leading to an increase in cognitive complexity. Information about Cognitive complexity can be found in the original paper here: <https://www.sonarsource.com/docs/CognitiveComplexity.pdf> By default, this rule reports methods with a complexity of 15 or more. Reported methods should be broken down into less complex components.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#cognitivecomplexity

Decision points are places where the control flow jumps to another place in the program. As such, they include all control flow statements, such as `if`, `while`, `for`, and `case`. For more details on the calculation, see the documentation {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#CYCLO %}. Generally, numbers ranging from 1-4 denote low complexity, 5-7 denote moderate complexity, 8-10 denote high complexity, and 11+ is very high complexity. By default, this rule reports methods with a complexity >= 10. Additionally, classes with many methods of moderate complexity get reported as well once the total of their methods' complexities reaches 80, even if none of the methods was directly reported. src\main\java\org\json\JSONML.java:87
  Code smell: NPathComplexity
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: The method 'parse(XMLTokener, boolean, JSONArray, JSONMLParserConfiguration, int)' has an NPath complexity of 2920, current threshold is 200
  Rule explanation: The NPath complexity of a method is the number of acyclic execution paths through that method. While cyclomatic complexity counts the number of decision points in a method, NPath counts the number of full paths from the beginning to the end of the block of the method. That metric grows exponentially, as it multiplies the complexity of statements in the same block. For more details on the calculation, see the documentation {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#NPATH %}. A threshold of 200 is generally considered the point where measures should be taken to reduce complexity and increase readability.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#npathcomplexity

  Rule explanation: Reports unchecked exceptions in the `throws` clause of a method or constructor. A better practice is to document the exceptional cases with a `@throws` Javadoc tag, which allows being more descriptive.

src\main\java\org\json\JSONML.java:627
  Code smell: CognitiveComplexity
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: The method 'toString(JSONObject)' has a cognitive complexity of 23, current threshold is 15
  Rule explanation: Methods that are highly complex are difficult to read and more costly to maintain. If you include too much decisional logic within a single method, you make its behavior hard to understand and more difficult to modify. Cognitive complexity is a measure of how difficult it is for humans to read and understand a method. Code that contains a break in the control flow is more complex, whereas the use of language shorthands doesn't increase the level of complexity. Nested control flows can make a method more difficult to understand, with each additional nesting of the control flow leading to an increase in cognitive complexity. Information about Cognitive complexity can be found in the original paper here: <https://www.sonarsource.com/docs/CognitiveComplexity.pdf> By default, this rule reports methods with a complexity of 15 or more. Reported methods should be broken down into less complex components.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#cognitivecomplexity

src\main\java\org\json\JSONML.java:627
  Code smell: CyclomaticComplexity
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: The method 'toString(JSONObject)' has a cyclomatic complexity of 12.
  Rule explanation: The complexity of methods directly affects maintenance costs and readability. Concentrating too much decisional logic in a single method makes its behaviour hard to read and change. Cyclomatic complexity assesses the complexity of a method by counting the number of decision points in a method, plus one for the method entry. Decision points are places where the control flow jumps to another place in the program. As such, they include all control flow statements, such as `if`, `while`, `for`, and `case`. For more details on the calculation, see the documentation {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#CYCLO %}. Generally, numbers ranging from 1-4 denote low complexity, 5-7 denote moderate complexity, 8-10 denote high complexity, and 11+ is very high complexity. By default, this rule reports methods with a complexity >= 10. Additionally, classes with many methods of moderate complexity get reported as well once the total of their methods' complexities reaches 80, even if none of the methods was directly reported. Reported methods should be broken down into several smaller methods. Reported classes should probably be broken down into subcomponents.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#cyclomaticcomplexity

src\main\java\org\json\JSONObject.java:77
  Code smell: GodClass
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: Possible God Class (WMC=606, ATFD=124, TCC=1.480%)
  Rule explanation: The God Class rule detects the God Class design flaw using metrics. God classes do too many things, are very big and overly complex. They should be split apart to be more object-oriented. The rule uses the detection strategy described in "Object-Oriented Metrics in Practice". The violations are reported against the entire class. The rule uses metrics to implement its detection strategy. The violation message gives information about the values of these metrics: * WMC: a class complexity measure, see {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#WEIGHED_METHOD_COUNT %} * ATFD: a measure of how much data external data the class uses, see {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#ACCESS_TO_FOREIGN_DATA %} * TCC: a measure of how tightly related the methods are, see {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#TIGHT_CLASS_COHESION %} The rule identifies a god class by looking for classes which have all of the following properties: * High WMC * High ATFD * Low TCC See also the reference: Michele Lanza and Radu Marinescu. *Object-Oriented Metrics in Practice: Using Software Metrics to Characterize, Evaluate, and Improve the Design of Object-Oriented Systems.* Springer, Berlin, 1 edition, October 2006. Page 80.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#godclass

src\main\java\org\json\JSONObject.java:187
  Code smell: AvoidCatchingGenericException
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: Avoid catching Exception in try-catch block
  Rule explanation: Avoid catching generic exceptions in try-catch blocks. Catching overly broad exception types makes it difficult to understand what can actually go wrong in your code and can hide real problems. **Why these exceptions should not be caught:** * **Exception**: This is the base class for all checked exceptions. Catching it means you're handling all possible checked exceptions the same way, which is rarely appropriate and makes error handling less precise. * **RuntimeException**: These represent programming errors (like logic bugs) that should typically be fixed in code rather than caught and handled. Catching them can hide bugs that should be addressed during development. * **NullPointerException**: This usually indicates a programming error (accessing null references). Rather than catching it, code should be written to avoid null pointer dereferences through null checks or defensive programming. * **Throwable**: This is the superclass of all errors and exceptions. Catching it means you're trying to handle both recoverable exceptions and serious errors (like OutOfMemoryError) the same way, which is dangerous. * **Error**: These represent serious problems that applications should not try to handle (like OutOfMemoryError, StackOverflowError). Catching Error can prevent the JVM from properly terminating when it encounters unrecoverable situations. **Better approaches:** - Catch specific exception types that you can meaningfully handle - Use multiple catch blocks for different exception types that require different handling - Consider using defensive programming techniques to prevent exceptions rather than catching them
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_errorprone.html#avoidcatchinggenericexception

src\main\java\org\json\JSONObject.java:391
  Code smell: AvoidThrowingNullPointerException
  Priority: 1 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: Avoid throwing null pointer exceptions.
  Rule explanation: Avoid throwing NullPointerExceptions manually. These are confusing because most people will assume that the virtual machine threw it. To avoid a method being called with a null parameter, you may consider using an IllegalArgumentException instead, making it clearly seen as a programmer-initiated exception. However, there are better ways to handle this: >*Effective Java, 3rd Edition, Item 72: Favor the use of standard exceptions* > >Arguably, every erroneous method invocation boils down to an illegal argument or state, but other exceptions are standardly used for certain kinds of illegal arguments and states. If a caller passes null in some parameter for which null values are prohibited, convention dictates that NullPointerException be thrown rather than IllegalArgumentException. To implement that, you are encouraged to use `java.util.Objects.requireNonNull()` (introduced in Java 1.7). This method is designed primarily for doing parameter validation in methods and constructors with multiple parameters. Your parameter validation could thus look like the following: ``` public class Foo { private String exampleValue; void setExampleValue(String exampleValue) { // check, throw and assignment in a single standard call this.exampleValue = Objects.requireNonNull(exampleValue, "exampleValue must not be null!"); } } ```
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#avoidthrowingnullpointerexception

A better practice is to document the exceptional cases with a `@throws` Javadoc tag, which allows being more descriptive.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#avoiduncheckedexceptionsinsignatures

src\main\java\org\json\JSONObject.java:854
  Code smell: AvoidCatchingGenericException
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: Avoid catching Exception in try-catch block
  Rule explanation: Avoid catching generic exceptions in try-catch blocks. Catching overly broad exception types makes it difficult to understand what can actually go wrong in your code and can hide real problems. **Why these exceptions should not be caught:** * **Exception**: This is the base class for all checked exceptions. Catching it means you're handling all possible checked exceptions the same way, which is rarely appropriate and makes error handling less precise. * **RuntimeException**: These represent programming errors (like logic bugs) that should typically be fixed in code rather than caught and handled. Catching them can hide bugs that should be addressed during development. * **NullPointerException**: This usually indicates a programming error (accessing null references). Rather than catching it, code should be written to avoid null pointer dereferences through null checks or defensive programming. * **Throwable**: This is the superclass of all errors and exceptions. Catching it means you're trying to handle both recoverable exceptions and serious errors (like OutOfMemoryError) the same way, which is dangerous. * **Error**: These represent serious problems that applications should not try to handle (like OutOfMemoryError, StackOverflowError). Catching Error can prevent the JVM from properly terminating when it encounters unrecoverable situations. **Better approaches:** - Catch specific exception types that you can meaningfully handle - Use multiple catch blocks for different exception types that require different handling - Consider using defensive programming techniques to prevent exceptions rather than catching them
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_errorprone.html#avoidcatchinggenericexception

src\main\java\org\json\JSONObject.java:1338
  Code smell: CyclomaticComplexity
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: The method 'objectToBigDecimal(Object, BigDecimal, boolean)' has a cyclomatic complexity of 13.
  Rule explanation: The complexity of methods directly affects maintenance costs and readability. Concentrating too much decisional logic in a single method makes its behaviour hard to read and change. Cyclomatic complexity assesses the complexity of a method by counting the number of decision points in a method, plus one for the method entry. Decision points are places where the control flow jumps to another place in the program. As such, they include all control flow statements, such as `if`, `while`, `for`, and `case`. For more details on the calculation, see the documentation {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#CYCLO %}. Generally, numbers ranging from 1-4 denote low complexity, 5-7 denote moderate complexity, 8-10 denote high complexity, and 11+ is very high complexity. By default, this rule reports methods with a complexity >= 10. Additionally, classes with many methods of moderate complexity get reported as well once the total of their methods' complexities reaches 80, even if none of the methods was directly reported. Reported methods should be broken down into several smaller methods. Reported classes should probably be broken down into subcomponents.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#cyclomaticcomplexity

src\main\java\org\json\JSONObject.java:1930
  Code smell: CognitiveComplexity
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: The method 'getKeyNameFromMethod(Method, boolean)' has a cognitive complexity of 18, current threshold is 15
  Rule explanation: Methods that are highly complex are difficult to read and more costly to maintain. If you include too much decisional logic within a single method, you make its behavior hard to understand and more difficult to modify. Cognitive complexity is a measure of how difficult it is for humans to read and understand a method. Code that contains a break in the control flow is more complex, whereas the use of language shorthands doesn't increase the level of complexity. Nested control flows can make a method more difficult to understand, with each additional nesting of the control flow leading to an increase in cognitive complexity. Information about Cognitive complexity can be found in the original paper here: <https://www.sonarsource.com/docs/CognitiveComplexity.pdf> By default, this rule reports methods with a complexity of 15 or more. Reported methods should be broken down into less complex components.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#cognitivecomplexity

  Why: A method or constructor should not explicitly declare unchecked exception JSONException in its 'throws' clause
  Rule explanation: Reports unchecked exceptions in the `throws` clause of a method or constructor. Java doesn't force the caller to handle an unchecked exception, so it's unnecessary except for documentation. A better practice is to document the exceptional cases with a `@throws` Javadoc tag, which allows being more descriptive.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#avoiduncheckedexceptionsinsignatures

src\main\java\org\json\JSONObject.java:2303
  Code smell: AvoidThrowingNullPointerException
  Priority: 1 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: Avoid throwing null pointer exceptions.
  Rule explanation: Avoid throwing NullPointerExceptions manually. These are confusing because most people will assume that the virtual machine threw it. To avoid a method being called with a null parameter, you may consider using an IllegalArgumentException instead, making it clearly seen as a programmer-initiated exception. However, there are better ways to handle this: >*Effective Java, 3rd Edition, Item 72: Favor the use of standard exceptions* > >Arguably, every erroneous method invocation boils down to an illegal argument or state, but other exceptions are standardly used for certain kinds of illegal arguments and states. If a caller passes null in some parameter for which null values are prohibited, convention dictates that NullPointerException be thrown rather than IllegalArgumentException. To implement that, you are encouraged to use `java.util.Objects.requireNonNull()` (introduced in Java 1.7). This method is designed primarily for doing parameter validation in methods and constructors with multiple parameters. Your parameter validation could thus look like the following: ``` public class Foo { private String exampleValue; void setExampleValue(String exampleValue) { // check, throw and assignment in a single standard call this.exampleValue = Objects.requireNonNull(exampleValue, "exampleValue must not be null!"); } } ```
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#avoidthrowingnullpointerexception

src\main\java\org\json\JSONObject.java:2464
  Code smell: CyclomaticComplexity
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: The method 'quote(String, Writer)' has a cyclomatic complexity of 13.
  Rule explanation: The complexity of methods directly affects maintenance costs and readability. Concentrating too much decisional logic in a single method makes its behaviour hard to read and change. Cyclomatic complexity assesses the complexity of a method by counting the number of decision points in a method, plus one for the method entry. Decision points are places where the control flow jumps to another place in the program. As such, they include all control flow statements, such as `if`, `while`, `for`, and `case`. For more details on the calculation, see the documentation {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#CYCLO %}. Generally, numbers ranging from 1-4 denote low complexity, 5-7 denote moderate complexity, 8-10 denote high complexity, and 11+ is very high complexity. By default, this rule reports methods with a complexity >= 10. Additionally, classes with many methods of moderate complexity get reported as well once the total of their methods' complexities reaches 80, even if none of the methods was directly reported. Reported methods should be broken down into several smaller methods. Reported classes should probably be broken down into subcomponents.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#cyclomaticcomplexity

src\main\java\org\json\JSONObject.java:2558
  Code smell: SimplifyBooleanReturns
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: This if statement can be replaced by `return !{condition} && {elseBranch};`
  Rule explanation: Avoid unnecessary if-then-else statements when returning a boolean. The result of the conditional test can be returned instead.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#simplifybooleanreturns

src\main\java\org\json\JSONObject.java:2562
  Code smell: AvoidCatchingGenericException
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: Avoid catching Exception in try-catch block
  Rule explanation: Avoid catching generic exceptions in try-catch blocks. Catching overly broad exception types makes it difficult to understand what can actually go wrong in your code and can hide real problems. **Why these exceptions should not be caught:** * **Exception**: This is the base class for all checked exceptions. Catching it means you're handling all possible checked exceptions the same way, which is rarely appropriate and makes error handling less precise. * **RuntimeException**: These represent programming errors (like logic bugs) that should typically be fixed in code rather than caught and handled. Catching them can hide bugs that should be addressed during development. * **NullPointerException**: This usually indicates a programming error (accessing null references). Rather than catching it, code should be written to avoid null pointer dereferences through null checks or defensive programming. * **Throwable**: This is the superclass of all errors and exceptions. Catching it means you're trying to handle both recoverable exceptions and serious errors (like OutOfMemoryError) the same way, which is dangerous. * **Error**: These represent serious problems that applications should not try to handle (like OutOfMemoryError, StackOverflowError). Catching Error can prevent the JVM from properly terminating when it encounters unrecoverable situations. **Better approaches:** - Catch specific exception types that you can meaningfully handle - Use multiple catch blocks for different exception types that require different handling - Consider using defensive programming techniques to prevent exceptions rather than catching them
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_errorprone.html#avoidcatchinggenericexception

Catching Error can prevent the JVM from properly terminating when it encounters unrecoverable situations. **Better approaches:** - Catch specific exception types that you can meaningfully handle - Use multiple catch blocks for different exception types that require different handling - Consider using defensive programming techniques to prevent exceptions rather than catching them
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_errorprone.html#avoidcatchinggenericexception

  Why: Avoid catching Exception in try-catch block
  Rule explanation: Avoid catching generic exceptions in try-catch blocks. Catching overly broad exception types makes it difficult to understand what can actually go wrong in your code and can hide real problems. **Why these exceptions should not be caught:** * **Exception**: This is the base class for all checked exceptions.   Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_errorprone.html#avoidcatchinggenericexception

src\main\java\org\json\JSONObject.java:3393
  Code smell: CognitiveComplexity
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: The method 'convertValue(Object, Type)' has a cognitive complexity of 24, current threshold is 15
  Rule explanation: Methods that are highly complex are difficult to read and more costly to maintain. If you include too much decisional logic within a single method, you make its behavior hard to understand and more difficult to modify. Cognitive complexity is a measure of how difficult it is for humans to read and understand a method. Code that contains a break in the control flow is more complex, whereas the use of language shorthands doesn't increase the level of complexity. Nested control flows can make a method more difficult to understand, with each additional nesting of the control flow leading to an increase in cognitive complexity. Information about Cognitive complexity can be found in the original paper here: <https://www.sonarsource.com/docs/CognitiveComplexity.pdf> By default, this rule reports methods with a complexity of 15 or more. Reported methods should be broken down into less complex components.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#cognitivecomplexity

src\main\java\org\json\JSONObject.java:3393
  Code smell: CyclomaticComplexity
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: The method 'convertValue(Object, Type)' has a cyclomatic complexity of 25.
  Rule explanation: The complexity of methods directly affects maintenance costs and readability. Concentrating too much decisional logic in a single method makes its behaviour hard to read and change. Cyclomatic complexity assesses the complexity of a method by counting the number of decision points in a method, plus one for the method entry. Decision points are places where the control flow jumps to another place in the program. As such, they include all control flow statements, such as `if`, `while`, `for`, and `case`. For more details on the calculation, see the documentation {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#CYCLO %}. Generally, numbers ranging from 1-4 denote low complexity, 5-7 denote moderate complexity, 8-10 denote high complexity, and 11+ is very high complexity. By default, this rule reports methods with a complexity >= 10. Additionally, classes with many methods of moderate complexity get reported as well once the total of their methods' complexities reaches 80, even if none of the methods was directly reported. Reported methods should be broken down into several smaller methods. Reported classes should probably be broken down into subcomponents.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#cyclomaticcomplexity

src\main\java\org\json\JSONObject.java:3393
  Code smell: AvoidUncheckedExceptionsInSignatures
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: A method or constructor should not explicitly declare unchecked exception JSONException in its 'throws' clause
  Rule explanation: Reports unchecked exceptions in the `throws` clause of a method or constructor. Java doesn't force the caller to handle an unchecked exception, so it's unnecessary except for documentation. A better practice is to document the exceptional cases with a `@throws` Javadoc tag, which allows being more descriptive.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#avoiduncheckedexceptionsinsignatures

src\main\java\org\json\JSONPointer.java:77
  Code smell: AvoidThrowingNullPointerException
  Priority: 1 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: Avoid throwing null pointer exceptions.
  Rule explanation: Avoid throwing NullPointerExceptions manually. These are confusing because most people will assume that the virtual machine threw it. To avoid a method being called with a null parameter, you may consider using an IllegalArgumentException instead, making it clearly seen as a programmer-initiated exception. However, there are better ways to handle this: >*Effective Java, 3rd Edition, Item 72: Favor the use of standard exceptions* > >Arguably, every erroneous method invocation boils down to an illegal argument or state, but other exceptions are standardly used for certain kinds of illegal arguments and states. If a caller passes null in some parameter for which null values are prohibited, convention dictates that NullPointerException be thrown rather than IllegalArgumentException. To implement that, you are encouraged to use `java.util.Objects.requireNonNull()` (introduced in Java 1.7). This method is designed primarily for doing parameter validation in methods and constructors with multiple parameters. Your parameter validation could thus look like the following: ``` public class Foo { private String exampleValue; void setExampleValue(String exampleValue) { // check, throw and assignment in a single standard call this.exampleValue = Objects.requireNonNull(exampleValue, "exampleValue must not be null!"); } } ```
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#avoidthrowingnullpointerexception

  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#avoiduncheckedexceptionsinsignatures

src\main\java\org\json\JSONTokener.java:476
  Code smell: CognitiveComplexity
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: The method 'nextSimpleValue(char)' has a cognitive complexity of 17, current threshold is 15
  Rule explanation: Methods that are highly complex are difficult to read and more costly to maintain. If you include too much decisional logic within a single method, you make its behavior hard to understand and more difficult to modify. Cognitive complexity is a measure of how difficult it is for humans to read and understand a method. Code that contains a break in the control flow is more complex, whereas the use of language shorthands doesn't increase the level of complexity. Nested control flows can make a method more difficult to understand, with each additional nesting of the control flow leading to an increase in cognitive complexity. Information about Cognitive complexity can be found in the original paper here: <https://www.sonarsource.com/docs/CognitiveComplexity.pdf> By default, this rule reports methods with a complexity of 15 or more. Reported methods should be broken down into less complex components.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#cognitivecomplexity

src\main\java\org\json\JSONTokener.java:476
  Code smell: CyclomaticComplexity
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: The method 'nextSimpleValue(char)' has a cyclomatic complexity of 27.
  Rule explanation: The complexity of methods directly affects maintenance costs and readability. Concentrating too much decisional logic in a single method makes its behaviour hard to read and change. Cyclomatic complexity assesses the complexity of a method by counting the number of decision points in a method, plus one for the method entry. Decision points are places where the control flow jumps to another place in the program. As such, they include all control flow statements, such as `if`, `while`, `for`, and `case`. For more details on the calculation, see the documentation {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#CYCLO %}. Generally, numbers ranging from 1-4 denote low complexity, 5-7 denote moderate complexity, 8-10 denote high complexity, and 11+ is very high complexity. By default, this rule reports methods with a complexity >= 10. Additionally, classes with many methods of moderate complexity get reported as well once the total of their methods' complexities reaches 80, even if none of the methods was directly reported. Reported methods should be broken down into several smaller methods. Reported classes should probably be broken down into subcomponents.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#cyclomaticcomplexity

src\main\java\org\json\JSONTokener.java:476
  Code smell: NPathComplexity
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: The method 'nextSimpleValue(char)' has an NPath complexity of 1015, current threshold is 200
  Rule explanation: The NPath complexity of a method is the number of acyclic execution paths through that method. While cyclomatic complexity counts the number of decision points in a method, NPath counts the number of full paths from the beginning to the end of the block of the method. That metric grows exponentially, as it multiplies the complexity of statements in the same block. For more details on the calculation, see the documentation {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#NPATH %}. A threshold of 200 is generally considered the point where measures should be taken to reduce complexity and increase readability.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#npathcomplexity

src\main\java\org\json\JSONWriter.java:312
  Code smell: AvoidCatchingGenericException
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: Avoid catching Exception in try-catch block
  Rule explanation: Avoid catching generic exceptions in try-catch blocks. Catching overly broad exception types makes it difficult to understand what can actually go wrong in your code and can hide real problems. **Why these exceptions should not be caught:** * **Exception**: This is the base class for all checked exceptions. Catching it means you're handling all possible checked exceptions the same way, which is rarely appropriate and makes error handling less precise. * **RuntimeException**: These represent programming errors (like logic bugs) that should typically be fixed in code rather than caught and handled. Catching them can hide bugs that should be addressed during development. * **NullPointerException**: This usually indicates a programming error (accessing null references). Rather than catching it, code should be written to avoid null pointer dereferences through null checks or defensive programming. * **Throwable**: This is the superclass of all errors and exceptions. Catching it means you're trying to handle both recoverable exceptions and serious errors (like OutOfMemoryError) the same way, which is dangerous. * **Error**: These represent serious problems that applications should not try to handle (like OutOfMemoryError, StackOverflowError). Catching Error can prevent the JVM from properly terminating when it encounters unrecoverable situations. **Better approaches:** - Catch specific exception types that you can meaningfully handle - Use multiple catch blocks for different exception types that require different handling - Consider using defensive programming techniques to prevent exceptions rather than catching them
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_errorprone.html#avoidcatchinggenericexception

src\main\java\org\json\XML.java:22
  Code smell: GodClass
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: Possible God Class (WMC=202, ATFD=27, TCC=0.000%)
  Rule explanation: The God Class rule detects the God Class design flaw using metrics. God classes do too many things, are very big and overly complex. They should be split apart to be more object-oriented. The rule uses the detection strategy described in "Object-Oriented Metrics in Practice". The violations are reported against the entire class. The rule uses metrics to implement its detection strategy. The violation message gives information about the values of these metrics: * WMC: a class complexity measure, see {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#WEIGHED_METHOD_COUNT %} * ATFD: a measure of how much data external data the class uses, see {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#ACCESS_TO_FOREIGN_DATA %} * TCC: a measure of how tightly related the methods are, see {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#TIGHT_CLASS_COHESION %} The rule identifies a god class by looking for classes which have all of the following properties: * High WMC * High ATFD * Low TCC See also the reference: Michele Lanza and Radu Marinescu. *Object-Oriented Metrics in Practice: Using Software Metrics to Characterize, Evaluate, and Improve the Design of Object-Oriented Systems.* Springer, Berlin, 1 edition, October 2006. Page 80.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#godclass

  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#cognitivecomplexity

src\main\java\org\json\XML.java:251
  Code smell: CyclomaticComplexity
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: The method 'parse(XMLTokener, JSONObject, String, XMLParserConfiguration, int)' has a cyclomatic complexity of 79.
  Rule explanation: The complexity of methods directly affects maintenance costs and readability. Concentrating too much decisional logic in a single method makes its behaviour hard to read and change. Cyclomatic complexity assesses the complexity of a method by counting the number of decision points in a method, plus one for the method entry. Decision points are places where the control flow jumps to another place in the program. As such, they include all control flow statements, such as `if`, `while`, `for`, and `case`. For more details on the calculation, see the documentation {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#CYCLO %}. Generally, numbers ranging from 1-4 denote low complexity, 5-7 denote moderate complexity, 8-10 denote high complexity, and 11+ is very high complexity. By default, this rule reports methods with a complexity >= 10. Additionally, classes with many methods of moderate complexity get reported as well once the total of their methods' complexities reaches 80, even if none of the methods was directly reported. Reported methods should be broken down into several smaller methods. Reported classes should probably be broken down into subcomponents.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#cyclomaticcomplexity

src\main\java\org\json\XML.java:251
  Code smell: NPathComplexity
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: The method 'parse(XMLTokener, JSONObject, String, XMLParserConfiguration, int)' has an NPath complexity of 215, current threshold is 200
  Rule explanation: The NPath complexity of a method is the number of acyclic execution paths through that method. While cyclomatic complexity counts the number of decision points in a method, NPath counts the number of full paths from the beginning to the end of the block of the method. That metric grows exponentially, as it multiplies the complexity of statements in the same block. For more details on the calculation, see the documentation {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#NPATH %}. A threshold of 200 is generally considered the point where measures should be taken to reduce complexity and increase readability.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#npathcomplexity

  Why: A method or constructor should not explicitly declare unchecked exception JSONException in its 'throws' clause
  Rule explanation: Reports unchecked exceptions in the `throws` clause of a method or constructor. Java doesn't force the caller to handle an unchecked exception, so it's unnecessary except for documentation. A better practice is to document the exceptional cases with a `@throws` Javadoc tag, which allows being more descriptive.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#avoiduncheckedexceptionsinsignatures

  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: The method 'removeEmpty(JSONObject, XMLParserConfiguration)' has a cognitive complexity of 22, current threshold is 15
  Rule explanation: Methods that are highly complex are difficult to read and more costly to maintain. If you include too much decisional logic within a single method, you make its behavior hard to understand and more difficult to modify. Cognitive complexity is a measure of how difficult it is for humans to read and understand a method. Code that contains a break in the control flow is more complex, whereas the use of language shorthands doesn't increase the level of complexity. Nested control flows can make a method more difficult to understand, with each additional nesting of the control flow leading to an increase in cognitive complexity. Reported methods should be broken down into less complex components.

src\main\java\org\json\XML.java:654
  Code smell: AvoidCatchingGenericException
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: Avoid catching Exception in try-catch block
  Rule explanation: Avoid catching generic exceptions in try-catch blocks. Catching overly broad exception types makes it difficult to understand what can actually go wrong in your code and can hide real problems. **Why these exceptions should not be caught:** * **Exception**: This is the base class for all checked exceptions. Catching it means you're handling all possible checked exceptions the same way, which is rarely appropriate and makes error handling less precise. * **RuntimeException**: These represent programming errors (like logic bugs) that should typically be fixed in code rather than caught and handled. Catching them can hide bugs that should be addressed during development. * **NullPointerException**: This usually indicates a programming error (accessing null references). Rather than catching it, code should be written to avoid null pointer dereferences through null checks or defensive programming. * **Throwable**: This is the superclass of all errors and exceptions. Catching it means you're trying to handle both recoverable exceptions and serious errors (like OutOfMemoryError) the same way, which is dangerous. * **Error**: These represent serious problems that applications should not try to handle (like OutOfMemoryError, StackOverflowError). Catching Error can prevent the JVM from properly terminating when it encounters unrecoverable situations. **Better approaches:** - Catch specific exception types that you can meaningfully handle - Use multiple catch blocks for different exception types that require different handling - Consider using defensive programming techniques to prevent exceptions rather than catching them
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_errorprone.html#avoidcatchinggenericexception

src\main\java\org\json\XMLTokener.java:15
  Code smell: CyclomaticComplexity
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: The class 'XMLTokener' has a total cyclomatic complexity of 135 (highest 32).
  Rule explanation: The complexity of methods directly affects maintenance costs and readability. Concentrating too much decisional logic in a single method makes its behaviour hard to read and change. Cyclomatic complexity assesses the complexity of a method by counting the number of decision points in a method, plus one for the method entry. Decision points are places where the control flow jumps to another place in the program. As such, they include all control flow statements, such as `if`, `while`, `for`, and `case`. For more details on the calculation, see the documentation {% jdoc java::lang.java.metrics.JavaMetrics#CYCLO %}. Generally, numbers ranging from 1-4 denote low complexity, 5-7 denote moderate complexity, 8-10 denote high complexity, and 11+ is very high complexity. By default, this rule reports methods with a complexity >= 10. Additionally, classes with many methods of moderate complexity get reported as well once the total of their methods' complexities reaches 80, even if none of the methods was directly reported. Reported methods should be broken down into several smaller methods. Reported classes should probably be broken down into subcomponents.
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#cyclomaticcomplexity

Reported methods should be broken down into less complex components.

src\test\java\org\json\junit\EnumTest.java:349
  Code smell: AvoidCatchingGenericException
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: Avoid catching Exception in try-catch block
  Rule explanation: Avoid catching generic exceptions in try-catch blocks. Catching overly broad exception types makes it difficult to understand what can actually go wrong in your code and can hide real problems. **Why these exceptions should not be caught:** * **Exception**: This is the base class for all checked exceptions. Catching it means you're handling all possible checked exceptions the same way, which is rarely appropriate and makes error handling less precise. * **RuntimeException**: These represent programming errors (like logic bugs) that should typically be fixed in code rather than caught and handled. Catching them can hide bugs that should be addressed during development. * **NullPointerException**: This usually indicates a programming error (accessing null references). Rather than catching it, code should be written to avoid null pointer dereferences through null checks or defensive programming. * **Throwable**: This is the superclass of all errors and exceptions. Catching it means you're trying to handle both recoverable exceptions and serious errors (like OutOfMemoryError) the same way, which is dangerous. * **Error**: These represent serious problems that applications should not try to handle (like OutOfMemoryError, StackOverflowError). Catching Error can prevent the JVM from properly terminating when it encounters unrecoverable situations. **Better approaches:** - Catch specific exception types that you can meaningfully handle - Use multiple catch blocks for different exception types that require different handling - Consider using defensive programming techniques to prevent exceptions rather than catching them
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_errorprone.html#avoidcatchinggenericexception

src\test\java\org\json\junit\EnumTest.java:353
  Code smell: LawOfDemeter
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: Call to `getMyEnum` on foreign value `actualEnumClass` (degree 1)
  Rule explanation: The law of Demeter is a simple rule that says "only talk to friends". It forbids fetching data from "too far away", for some definition of distance, in order to reduce coupling between classes or objects of different levels of abstraction. The rule uses a notion of "degree", that quantifies how "far" an object is. Expressions with too high degree can only be used in certain ways. The degree of an expression is defined inductively: - The degree of `this` is 0 - The degree of a method parameter is 1 - The degree of a new object created in a method is 1 - The degree of a static variable is 1 - The degree of a field access expression like `expr.field` is the degree of `expr` plus 1 - The degree of a "getter expression" like `expr.getFoo()` is the degree of `expr` plus 1 - The degree of a "transformation expression" like `expr.withFoo("")` is the degree of `expr` - The degree of a variable is the maximum degree of all the assignments that reach it Intuitively, the more you call getters, the more the degree increases. Eventually the degree reaches the report threshold (property `trustRadius`) and the expression is reported. The details of the calculation are more involved and make room for common patterns, like usage of collections (objects that are in a list or array have the same degree as their container), the builder pattern, and getters that do not appear to break a boundary of abstraction. Be aware that this rule is prone to many false-positives and low-priority warnings. You can increase the `trustRadius` property to reduce them drastically. The default `trustRadius` of 1 corresponds to the original law of Demeter (you're only allowed one getter call on untrusted values). Given some `trustRadius` value: - expressions of degree lower or equal to `trustRadius` are not reported - expressions of degree exactly `trustRadius + 1` are reported, unless they are only returned from the current method, or passed as argument to another method. Without this exception it would not be possible to extract any information from e.g. method parameters. - values of degree strictly greater than `trustRadius + 1` are not reported. The intuition is that to obtain a value of degree `n > 1` then you must use an expression of degree `n - 1`, so if you have `n > trustRadius + 1`, there you're using some value of degree `trustRadius + 1` that will be reported. See also the references: * Andrew Hunt, David Thomas, and Ward Cunningham. The Pragmatic Programmer. From Journeyman to Master. Addison-Wesley Longman, Amsterdam, October 1999.; * K.J. Lieberherr and I.M. Holland. Assuring good style for object-oriented programs. Software, IEEE, 6(5):38–48, 1989.; * <http://www.ccs.neu.edu/home/lieber/LoD.html> * <http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_Demeter>
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#lawofdemeter

src\test\java\org\json\junit\EnumTest.java:365
  Code smell: LawOfDemeter
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: Call to `getMyEnum` on foreign value `actualEnumClass` (degree 1)
  Rule explanation: The law of Demeter is a simple rule that says "only talk to friends". It forbids fetching data from "too far away", for some definition of distance, in order to reduce coupling between classes or objects of different levels of abstraction. The rule uses a notion of "degree", that quantifies how "far" an object is. Expressions with too high degree can only be used in certain ways. The degree of an expression is defined inductively: - The degree of `this` is 0 - The degree of a method parameter is 1 - The degree of a new object created in a method is 1 - The degree of a static variable is 1 - The degree of a field access expression like `expr.field` is the degree of `expr` plus 1 - The degree of a "getter expression" like `expr.getFoo()` is the degree of `expr` plus 1 - The degree of a "transformation expression" like `expr.withFoo("")` is the degree of `expr` - The degree of a variable is the maximum degree of all the assignments that reach it Intuitively, the more you call getters, the more the degree increases. Eventually the degree reaches the report threshold (property `trustRadius`) and the expression is reported. The details of the calculation are more involved and make room for common patterns, like usage of collections (objects that are in a list or array have the same degree as their container), the builder pattern, and getters that do not appear to break a boundary of abstraction. Be aware that this rule is prone to many false-positives and low-priority warnings. You can increase the `trustRadius` property to reduce them drastically. The default `trustRadius` of 1 corresponds to the original law of Demeter (you're only allowed one getter call on untrusted values). Given some `trustRadius` value: - expressions of degree lower or equal to `trustRadius` are not reported - expressions of degree exactly `trustRadius + 1` are reported, unless they are only returned from the current method, or passed as argument to another method. Without this exception it would not be possible to extract any information from e.g. method parameters. - values of degree strictly greater than `trustRadius + 1` are not reported. The intuition is that to obtain a value of degree `n > 1` then you must use an expression of degree `n - 1`, so if you have `n > trustRadius + 1`, there you're using some value of degree `trustRadius + 1` that will be reported. See also the references: * Andrew Hunt, David Thomas, and Ward Cunningham. The Pragmatic Programmer. From Journeyman to Master. Addison-Wesley Longman, Amsterdam, October 1999.; * K.J. Lieberherr and I.M. Holland. Assuring good style for object-oriented programs. Software, IEEE, 6(5):38–48, 1989.; * <http://www.ccs.neu.edu/home/lieber/LoD.html> * <http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_Demeter>
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_design.html#lawofdemeter

src\test\java\org\json\junit\EnumTest.java:402
  Code smell: AvoidCatchingGenericException
  Priority: 3 (1 highest, 5 lowest)
  Why: Avoid catching Exception in try-catch block
  Rule explanation: Avoid catching generic exceptions in try-catch blocks. Catching overly broad exception types makes it difficult to understand what can actually go wrong in your code and can hide real problems. **Why these exceptions should not be caught:** * **Exception**: This is the base class for all checked exceptions. Catching it means you're handling all possible checked exceptions the same way, which is rarely appropriate and makes error handling less precise. * **RuntimeException**: These represent programming errors (like logic bugs) that should typically be fixed in code rather than caught and handled. Catching them can hide bugs that should be addressed during development. * **NullPointerException**: This usually indicates a programming error (accessing null references). Rather than catching it, code should be written to avoid null pointer dereferences through null checks or defensive programming. * **Throwable**: This is the superclass of all errors and exceptions. Catching it means you're trying to handle both recoverable exceptions and serious errors (like OutOfMemoryError) the same way, which is dangerous. * **Error**: These represent serious problems that applications should not try to handle (like OutOfMemoryError, StackOverflowError). Catching Error can prevent the JVM from properly terminating when it encounters unrecoverable situations. **Better approaches:** - Catch specific exception types that you can meaningfully handle - Use multiple catch blocks for different exception types that require different handling - Consider using defensive programming techniques to prevent exceptions rather than catching them
  Docs: https://docs.pmd-code.org/pmd-doc-7.21.0/pmd_rules_java_errorprone.html#avoidcatchinggenericexception
